The given function is \(\frac{x^3 - 1}{x - 1}\).

When \(x\) approaches 1, both the numerator and denominator approach zero, resulting in an indeterminate form of \(\frac{0}{0}\). To resolve this, factor the numerator:

\[
x^3 - 1 = (x - 1)(x^2 + x + 1)
\]

Canceling the common factor of \(x - 1\) gives:

\[
\frac{(x - 1)(x^2 + x + 1)}{x - 1} = x^2 + x + 1
\]

Now, compute the limit as \(x\) approaches 1:

\[
\lim_{x \to 1} (x^2 + x + 1) = 1^2 + 1 + 1 = 3
\]

First, observe that directly substituting \(x = 2\) results in a 0/0 indeterminate form. To resolve this, factor the numerator:

\[
x^2 - 4 = (x - 2)(x + 2)
\]

Substituting back into the expression gives:

\[
\frac{(x - 2)(x + 2)}{x - 2}
\]

Canceling the common \((x - 2)\) term simplifies to \(x + 2\). Taking the limit as \(x\) approaches 2:

\[
\lim_{{x \to 2}} (x + 2) = 4
\]

Thus, the final answer is:

The given expression is:

\[
\lim_{{x \to 1}} \frac{\sqrt[3]{(x+1)^3} - 1}{x-1}
\]

First, simplify the numerator. Notice that \( (x+1)^3 = x^3 + 3x^2 + 3x + 1 \), so:

\[
\sqrt[3]{(x+1)^3} = x + 1
\]

Thus, the expression becomes:

\[
\frac{(x + 1) - 1}{x - 1} = \frac{x}{x - 1}
\]

Now evaluate the limit as \( x \) approaches 1. When approaching from the right (\( x \to 1^+ \)), the denominator is a small positive number, so:

\[
\lim_{{x \to 1^+}} \frac{x}{x - 1} = +\infty
\]

When approaching from the left (\( x \to 1^- \)), the denominator is a small negative number, resulting in:

\[
\lim_{{x \to 1^-}} \frac{x}{x - 1} = -\infty
\]

Since the left and right limits are not equal, the two-sided limit does not exist.

**Answer:**

The limit does not exist because it approaches \(+\infty\) from the right and \(-\infty\) from the left. Therefore,

\[
\boxed{\text{DNE}}
\]

To find the **derivative** of the function \( f(x) = 3x^2 - 5x + 4 \), we will apply basic differentiation rules term by term.

### Step-by-Step Explanation:

1. **Differentiate each term separately:**
   
   - **First Term (\(3x^2\)):**
     \[
     \frac{d}{dx}(3x^2) = 3 \times 2x^{2-1} = 6x
     \]
     
   - **Second Term (\(-5x\)):**
     \[
     \frac{d}{dx}(-5x) = -5 \times 1 = -5
     \]
     
   - **Third Term (\(4\)):**
     \[
     \frac{d}{dx}(4) = 0
     \]

2. **Combine the derivatives of each term:**
   
   \[
   f'(x) = 6x - 5 + 0 = 6x - 5
   \]
   
### Final Answer:
\[
\boxed{f'(x) = 6x - 5}
\]

For functions like e^x, there's a nice property that makes taking derivatives easy because of their exponential nature.

I recall that when you take the derivative of e raised to some function, say u(x), the derivative is e^u times the derivative of u. That's called the chain rule. So if I have e^(something), its derivative will be e^(something) multiplied by the derivative of that something. But in this case, my function is just e^x, which means u(x) = x.

Applying the chain rule here, the derivative should be e^x times the derivative of x with respect to x. The derivative of x with respect to x is 1 because the slope of x is always 1. So putting it all together, that would make the derivative of e^x equal to e^x multiplied by 1, which simplifies to just e^x.

Wait a second, I think sometimes people get confused between derivatives of e^x and other exponentials like a^x where a is not e. For example, if it's a constant raised to x, the derivative would be ln(a) times a^x. But since here a is e, and ln(e) is 1, that simplifies things further. So indeed, the derivative of e^x is e^x.

lim(h→0) [e^(x+h) - e^x]/h
= lim(h→0) [e^x * e^h - e^x] / h
= e^x * lim(h→0) [e^h - 1]/h

Now, I remember that the limit of (e^h - 1)/h as h approaches 0 is equal to 1. This is a standard limit in calculus. So substituting this back in, we get:

e^x * 1 = e^x

That confirms it again! The derivative is indeed e^x.

\[
H'(x) = u'(x) \cdot v(x) + u(x) \cdot v'(x)
\]

Let's identify the parts of our function:
- Let \( u(x) = \cos x \)
- Let \( v(x) = \ln x \)

\[

\]

\[

\[ \frac{1}{y} \cdot \frac{dy}{dx} = \ln(x) + 1 \]

First, I identify u and v from the given function:

Next, I compute the derivatives of u and v:
The derivative of u with respect to x is du/dx = 1.
The derivative of v with respect to x is dv/dx = e^x.

Now, applying the product rule, which states that d/dx [u \cdot v] = du/dx \cdot v + u \cdot dv/dx:
dF/dx = (du/dx) \cdot v + u \cdot (dv/dx)
       = 1 \cdot e^x + x \cdot e^x
       = e^x (1 + x)

\[
h'(x) = u'(x) \cdot v(x) + u(x) \cdot v'(x)
\]

**Step 1: Identify \( u(x) \) and \( v(x) \)**

Let:
\[
u(x) = x \\
v(x) = e^x
\]

v'(x) = e^x

\[
F'(x) = 1 \cdot e^x + x \cdot e^x

To find the **second derivative** of \( F(x) = x^2 e^x \), we will follow these steps:

### Step 1: Find the First Derivative (\( F'(x) \))
We'll use the **product rule** for differentiation, which states:
\[
\frac{d}{dx}[u v] = u' v + u v'
\]
Let \( u = x^2 \) and \( v = e^x \).

1. Compute \( u' \):
\[
u' = \frac{d}{dx}(x^2) = 2x
\]

2. Compute \( v' \):
\[
v' = \frac{d}{dx}(e^x) = e^x
\]

3. Apply the product rule:
\[
F'(x) = u' v + u v' = 2x e^x + x^2 e^x
\]
Simplify:
\[
F'(x) = (2x + x^2) e^x
\]

### Step 2: Find the Second Derivative (\( F''(x) \))
Now, we'll differentiate \( F'(x) \) again using the product rule.

Let \( u = 2x + x^2 \) and \( v = e^x \).

1. Compute \( u' \):
\[
u' = \frac{d}{dx}(2x + x^2) = 2 + 2x
\]

2. Compute \( v' \):
\[
v' = \frac{d}{dx}(e^x) = e^x
\]

3. Apply the product rule:
\[
F''(x) = u' v + u v' = (2 + 2x) e^x + (2x + x^2) e^x
\]
Combine like terms:
\[
F''(x) = [2 + 2x + 2x + x^2] e^x = (x^2 + 4x + 2) e^x
\]

### Final Answer
\[
\boxed{(x^{2} + 4x + 2)e^{x}}
\]

The function given is \( F(x) = \frac{1}{x^2 + 1} \). To find its derivative, we can rewrite it as \( F(x) = (x^2 + 1)^{-1} \).

Using the chain rule:
- The outer function is \( u^{-1} \), whose derivative is \( -u^{-2} \).
- The inner function is \( u = x^2 + 1 \), whose derivative is \( 2x \).

Applying these, we get:
\[
F'(x) = -1 \cdot (x^2 + 1)^{-2} \cdot 2x = -\frac{2x}{(x^2 + 1)^2}
\]

   \( F(x) = \ln(x^2) \)

   \[
   F(x) = 2\ln|x|
   \]

To find the second derivative of \( F(x) = x^3 \ln x \), I'll start by finding the first derivative using the product rule. The first derivative is:

\( F'(x) = 3x^2 \ln x + x^3 \cdot \frac{1}{x} = 3x^2 \ln x + x^2 \)

Next, I'll differentiate \( F'(x) \) to find the second derivative. Differentiating each term separately:

The derivative of \( 3x^2 \ln x \) uses the product rule again:
\( 6x \ln x + 3x^2 \cdot \frac{1}{x} = 6x \ln x + 3x \)

</think>

Sure, let's solve the problem step by step.

**Given:**  
\( F(x) = x^3 \ln x \)

**Step 1: Find the First Derivative \( F'(x) \)**  

We'll use the product rule for differentiation, which states that if \( u(x) \) and \( v(x) \) are differentiable functions, then:
\[ (u \cdot v)' = u'v + uv' \]

Let:
- \( u = x^3 \)
- \( v = \ln x \)

Compute the derivatives:
- \( u' = 3x^2 \)
- \( v' = \frac{1}{x} \)

Apply the product rule:
\[
F'(x) = 3x^2 \cdot \ln x + x^3 \cdot \frac{1}{x} 
       = 3x^2 \ln x + x^2
\]

**Step 2: Find the Second Derivative \( F''(x) \)**  

Differentiate \( F'(x) \):
\[
F'(x) = 3x^2 \ln x + x^2 
\]
We'll differentiate each term separately.

1. Differentiate \( 3x^2 \ln x \) using the product rule again:
   - Let \( u = 3x^2 \)
   - Let \( v = \ln x \)
   
   Compute the derivatives:
   - \( u' = 6x \)
   - \( v' = \frac{1}{x} \)

   Apply the product rule:
   \[
   \frac{d}{dx}(3x^2 \ln x) = 6x \cdot \ln x + 3x^2 \cdot \frac{1}{x} 
                          = 6x \ln x + 3x
   \]

2. Differentiate \( x^2 \):
\[
\frac{d}{dx}(x^2) = 2x
\]

Combine the results:
\[
F''(x) = 6x \ln x + 3x + 2x 
        = 6x \ln x + 5x
\]

**Final Answer:**  
\boxed{6x \ln x + 5x}

1. **Identify Degrees**: Both numerator (degree 3) and denominator (degree 3) have the highest power of x.

2. **Divide Each Term by Highest Power**:
   - Numerator: \( \frac{5x^3}{x^3} + \frac{2x}{x^3} = 5 + \frac{2}{x^2} \)
   - Denominator: \( \frac{7x^3}{x^3} + \frac{3x^2}{x^3} = 7 + \frac{3}{x} \)

3. **Evaluate Limit**: As \( x \to \infty \), the terms with lower powers of x vanish.
   - \( \lim_{x\to\infty} 5 = 5 \)
   - \( \lim_{x\to\infty} \frac{2}{x^2} = 0 \)
   - \( \lim_{x\to\infty} 7 = 7 \)
   - \( \lim_{x\to\infty} \frac{3}{x} = 0 \)

4. **Compute the Result**: The limit becomes \( \frac{5 + 0}{7 + 0} = \frac{5}{7} \).

To find the derivative of the function \( f(x) = 3x^2 - 5x + 4 \), we'll use basic differentiation rules. Here's a step-by-step solution:

1. **Differentiate each term separately:**
   
   - The derivative of \( 3x^2 \):
     \[
     \frac{d}{dx}(3x^2) = 6x
     \]
     
   - The derivative of \( -5x \):
     \[
     \frac{d}{dx}(-5x) = -5
     \]
     
   - The derivative of the constant term \( 4 \):
     \[
     \frac{d}{dx}(4) = 0
     \]

2. **Combine the derivatives:**
   
   Adding up the derivatives of each term:
   \[
   f'(x) = 6x - 5 + 0 = 6x - 5
   \]
   
3. **Final Answer:**
   
   The derivative of \( f(x) = 3x^2 - 5x + 4 \) is:
   \[
   \boxed{6x - 5}
   \]

Differentiate \( f'(x) \) with respect to \( x \):

To find the derivative of \( F(x) = \cos x \cdot \ln x \), we will use the **product rule**. The product rule states that if you have a function which is the product of two functions, say \( u(x) \) and \( v(x) \), then the derivative is:

\[
(u \cdot v)' = u' \cdot v + u \cdot v'
\]

In this case:
- Let \( u(x) = \cos x \)
- Let \( v(x) = \ln x \)

First, find the derivatives of \( u(x) \) and \( v(x) \):
\[
u'(x) = -\sin x
\]
\[
v'(x) = \frac{1}{x}
\]

Now, apply the product rule:
\[
F'(x) = u'(x) \cdot v(x) + u(x) \cdot v'(x)
\]
\[
F'(x) = (-\sin x) \cdot \ln x + \cos x \cdot \left( \frac{1}{x} \right)
\]

Simplify the expression:
\[
F'(x) = -\sin x \cdot \ln x + \frac{\cos x}{x}
\]

**Final Answer:**
\[
\boxed{-\sin x \cdot \ln x + \dfrac{\cos x}{x}}
\]

The derivative of \( x^x \) with respect to \( x \) can be found using logarithmic differentiation. 

Let \( y = x^x \). Taking the natural logarithm of both sides gives:
\[
\ln(y) = x \cdot \ln(x)
\]

Differentiating both sides with respect to \( x \):
\[
\frac{1}{y} \cdot \frac{dy}{dx} = \ln(x) + 1
\]

Solving for \( \frac{dy}{dx} \):
\[
\frac{dy}{dx} = y \cdot (\ln(x) + 1)
\]

Substituting back \( y = x^x \):
\[
\frac{d}{dx}(x^x) = x^x (\ln(x) + 1)
\]

**Final Answer:**
The derivative of \( x^x \) with respect to \( x \) is \( x^x (\ln(x) + 1) \).

   - For \( 2x e^x \): Apply product rule again, resulting in \( 2 e^x + 2x e^x \)
   - For \( x^2 e^x \): Apply product rule, resulting in \( 2x e^x + x^2 e^x \)

   - Quadratic term: \( x^2 e^x \)

Final result:

To find the derivative of \( F(x) = \frac{1}{x^2 + 1} \), follow these steps:

**Step 1: Rewrite the Function Using Negative Exponents**

First, rewrite the function to make differentiation easier:
\[
F(x) = (x^2 + 1)^{-1}
\]

**Step 2: Apply the Chain Rule**

The chain rule states that if \( F(x) = f(g(x)) \), then \( F'(x) = f'(g(x)) \cdot g'(x) \).

Here, let:
- \( f(u) = u^{-1} \)
- \( g(x) = x^2 + 1 \)

\[
f'(u) = -u^{-2}
\]
\[
g'(x) = 2x
\]

**Step 3: Multiply the Derivatives Together**

Using the chain rule:
\[
F'(x) = f'(g(x)) \cdot g'(x) = - (x^2 + 1)^{-2} \cdot 2x
\]

**Step 4: Simplify the Expression**

Simplify \( F'(x) \):
\[
F'(x) = -\frac{2x}{(x^2 + 1)^2}
\]

\[
\boxed{-\dfrac{2x}{(x^{2}+1)^{2}}}

The power rule says that if you have a function f(x) = x^n, where n is any real number, then the derivative f'(x) is n*x^(n-1). So in this case, our function is x squared. Let me write that down: F(x) = x².

Wait a second, did I remember the steps correctly? Let me double-check. The power rule: bring down the exponent as a coefficient, then reduce the exponent by one. So yeah, that's exactly what I did here. 2 comes down from the exponent 2, and then x becomes x to the first power, which is just x.

To find the derivative of f(x) = ln(sin x), follow these steps:

1. Let u = sin(x). This is the inner function.
2. The outer function is ln(u).

To determine the derivative of f(x) = ln(sin x):

- Recognize that this is a composite function, so use the chain rule.
- Let u = sin(x), which means du/dx = cos(x).
- The outer function becomes ln(u), whose derivative is 1/u.
- Multiply the derivatives: d/dx [ln(sin x)] = (1/u) * du/dx = (1/sin(x)) * cos(x) = cos(x)/sin(x).

{Final Answer: "cos(x)/sin(x)"}

To compute f'(x), where f(x) = ln(sin x):

1. Use the chain rule since there's a composition of functions.
2. The outer function is the natural logarithm, whose derivative with respect to its argument (let’s call it u) is 1/u.
3. The inner function is sin(x). Its derivative with respect to x is cos(x).
4. Multiply the derivatives together: f'(x) = (1/u) * du/dx = (1/sin(x)) * cos(x) = cos(x)/sin(x).

{Final Answer: "cos(x)/sin(x)"}

- Multiply by the derivative of u (sin(x)) which is cos(x).
- Thus, f'(x) = (cos(x)/sin(x)).

Step-by-step explanation:

To find f’(x):

- The function is a composition of two functions: outer function ln(u) and inner function u = sin x.
- Apply the chain rule which states that d/dx [f(g(x))] = f’(g(x)) * g’(x).
- Compute derivative of the outer function with respect to u:
  - If f(u)=ln(u), then f’(u)=1/u.
- Compute derivative of the inner function with respect to x:
  - Since u=sin x, du/dx = cos x.
- Multiply these derivatives together:
  - (1/u) * (du/dx) = (1/sin x)*(cos x).
- Simplify to get: cot(x), which is equivalent to cos x / sin x.

1. The first step is finding df/du= 1/u.
2. Then compute du/dx= cos x.
3. Multiply to get (df/dx)=cos x / sin x.

Final Answer:
{Final Answer: "cos(x)/sin(x)"}

Step-by-step explanation:

To determine the derivative of ln(sin x):

- Let u = sin x, f(u) = ln(u).
1. df/du= 1/u.
2. du/dx= cos x.
3. Multiply derivatives to get (df/dx)=cos x / sin x.

- Let u = sin x, so that f(u) = ln(u).
1. Compute df/du= 1/u.
2. Find du/dx= cos x.
3. Multiply these to obtain (df/dx)=cos x / sin x.

1. The first step is to find df/du= 1/u.
2. Then du/dx= cos x.
3. Multiply these derivatives: (df/dx)=cos x / sin x.

Final Answer:
{Final Answer: "cos(x)/sin(x)"}

1. The first step is to find df/du= 1/u.
2. Then du/dx= cos x.
3. Multiply these derivatives: (df/dx)=cos x / sin x.

Final Answer:
{Final Answer: "cos(x)/sin(x)"}

First, let me compute the first derivative, F'(x). The product rule states that if you have a function h(x) = u(x)v(x), then h'(x) = u'(x)v(x) + u(x)v'(x). So here, u(x) is x³ and v(x) is ln x.

Calculating u'(x): the derivative of x³ is 3x². Okay, that's straightforward.

Now, v'(x): the derivative of ln x is 1/x. Got it.

Putting it all together: F'(x) = u'(x)v(x) + u(x)v'(x) = 3x² * ln x + x³ * (1/x). Simplifying that, the second term becomes x² because x³ divided by x is x². So F'(x) simplifies to 3x² ln x + x².

Now I need the second derivative, which means taking the derivative of F'(x). Let's write F'(x) again: 3x² ln x + x².

So we can split this into two parts: the first part is 3x² ln x and the second part is just x². We'll find their derivatives separately and then add them up.

Starting with the first part, d/dx [3x² ln x]. Again, this requires the product rule because it's a product of 3x² and ln x. 

Let’s denote u(x) = 3x² and v(x) = ln x. Then:

u'(x) = 6x (since derivative of 3x² is 6x)
v'(x) = 1/x

Applying the product rule: u'(x)v(x) + u(x)v'(x) = 6x * ln x + 3x² * (1/x). Simplifying that, the second term becomes 3x because 3x² divided by x is 3x. So this part gives us 6x ln x + 3x.

Now for the second part of F'(x), which is d/dx [x²]. The derivative of x² is straightforward: it's 2x.

So putting both derivatives together, the second derivative F''(x) = (6x ln x + 3x) + 2x. Now combine like terms: 3x and 2x add up to 5x. Therefore, F''(x) simplifies to 6x ln x + 5x.

Alternatively, this can be factored as x*(6 ln x + 5), but since the question just asks for the second derivative without specifying a particular form, either expression should be correct.

To find the derivative of \( \tan^{-1}(x) \), I recall that the derivative of the inverse tangent function is a standard result in calculus.

I know that the general formula for the derivative of \( \tan^{-1}(u) \) with respect to x, where u is a function of x, is given by:

\[
\frac{d}{dx} (\tan^{-1}(u)) = \frac{u'}{1 + u^2}
\]

In this case, the function inside the inverse tangent is simply \( u = x \), so its derivative \( u' \) with respect to x is 1.

Substituting these values into the formula:

\[
\frac{d}{dx} (\tan^{-1}(x)) = \frac{1}{1 + x^2}
\]

Therefore, the final answer is \( \frac{1}{1 + x^2} \).

To find the derivative of \( \tan^{-1}(x) \), we can use the standard result from calculus for the inverse tangent function.

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Understand the Function:**
   The function given is \( \tan^{-1}(x) \), which is also known as the arctangent function.

2. **Recall the Derivative Formula:**
   The derivative of \( \tan^{-1}(u) \) with respect to \( x \), where \( u \) is a function of \( x \), is given by:
   \[
   \frac{d}{dx} (\tan^{-1}(u)) = \frac{u'}{1 + u^2}
   \]
   Here, \( u' \) represents the derivative of \( u \) with respect to \( x \).

3. **Apply the Formula:**
   In this case, let \( u = x \). Then:
   - The derivative of \( u \) with respect to \( x \) is \( u' = 1 \).
   
4. **Compute the Derivative:**
   Substituting these values into the formula:
   \[
   \frac{d}{dx} (\tan^{-1}(x)) = \frac{1}{1 + x^2}
   \]

**Final Answer:**

\[
\boxed{\frac{1}{1 + x^{2}}}
\]

First, I need to find the derivative of the function F(x) = x ln x. To do this, I'll use differentiation rules.

I recognize that F(x) is a product of two functions: u(x) = x and v(x) = ln x. Therefore, I should apply the product rule for differentiation, which states that the derivative of a product of two functions is the derivative of the first function times the second plus the first function times the derivative of the second.

Next, I'll find the derivatives of u(x) and v(x). The derivative of u(x) = x with respect to x is du/dx = 1. For v(x) = ln x, the derivative is dv/dx = 1/x.

Now, applying the product rule: dF/dx = (du/dx) * v(x) + u(x) * (dv/dx). Substituting the derivatives and the original functions into this formula gives dF/dx = 1 * ln x + x * (1/x).

Simplifying the expression, I notice that x multiplied by (1/x) simplifies to 1. Therefore, the derivative becomes dF/dx = ln x + 1.

Finally, combining these results, the derivative of F(x) = x ln x is ln x plus one.
</think>

To find the derivative of \( F(x) = x \ln x \), we'll use the **product rule** for differentiation. The product rule states that if you have a function \( h(x) = u(x) \cdot v(x) \), then its derivative is:

\[
h'(x) = u'(x) \cdot v(x) + u(x) \cdot v'(x)
\]

In this case, let:
- \( u(x) = x \)
- \( v(x) = \ln x \)

First, find the derivatives of \( u(x) \) and \( v(x) \):
\[
u'(x) = \frac{d}{dx} (x) = 1
\]
\[
v'(x) = \frac{d}{dx} (\ln x) = \frac{1}{x}
\]

Now, apply the product rule:
\[
F'(x) = u'(x) \cdot v(x) + u(x) \cdot v'(x)
\]
\[
F'(x) = 1 \cdot \ln x + x \cdot \left( \frac{1}{x} \right )
\]
\[
F'(x) = \ln x + 1
\]

So, the derivative of \( F(x) = x \ln x \) is:

**Final Answer:**
\[
\boxed{\ln x + 1}
\]

The problem requires finding the derivative of the function \( \frac{\sin x}{x^2 + 1} \).

**Step-by-Step Solution:**

1. **Identify the Functions:**
   - Let \( u = \sin x \) (numerator)
   - Let \( v = x^2 + 1 \) (denominator)

2. **Find Their Derivatives:**
   - \( u' = \cos x \)
   - \( v' = 2x \)

3. **Apply the Quotient Rule:**
   The quotient rule states that if you have a function \( \frac{u}{v} \), its derivative is:
   \[
   \left(\frac{u}{v}\right)' = \frac{u'v - uv'}{v^2}
   \]
   
4. **Substitute the Values:**
   Plugging in \( u, v, u', v' \):
   \[
   \frac{\cos x (x^2 + 1) - \sin x \cdot 2x}{(x^2 + 1)^2}
   \]

5. **Simplify if Possible:**
   The expression is already simplified.

**Final Answer:**

\[
\boxed{\frac{\cos x(x^{2}+1)-2x\sin x}{(x^{2}+1)^{2}}}
\]

**Solution:**

We need to find the limit of \( 2x^2 - 5x + 1 \) as \( x \) approaches 3.

Since this is a polynomial function and all polynomials are continuous everywhere, we can directly substitute \( x = 3 \).

\[
\begin{align*}
\lim_{{x \to 3}} (2x^2 - 5x + 1) &= 2(3)^2 - 5(3) + 1 \\
&= 2(9) - 15 + 1 \\
&= 18 - 15 + 1 \\
&= 4
\end{align*}
\]

\[
\lim_{{x \to 0^+}} \frac{1}{x^2} \int_{0}^{x^2} \sqrt{t}\, dt

   First, evaluate the integral \( \int_{0}^{x^2} \sqrt{t}\, dt \). The antiderivative of \( \sqrt{t} = t^{1/2} \) is:
   \[
   \frac{2}{3} t^{3/2}
   \]
   Evaluating from 0 to \( x^2 \):
   \[
   \left. \frac{2}{3} t^{3/2} \right|_{0}^{x^2} = \frac{2}{3} (x^2)^{3/2} - 0 = \frac{2}{3} x^3
   \]

   Replace the integral with \( \frac{2}{3} x^3 \) in the original limit expression:
   \[
   \lim_{{x \to 0^+}} \frac{\frac{2}{3} x^3}{x^2}
   \]

   Simplify \( \frac{x^3}{x^2} = x \), so the expression becomes:
   \[
   \lim_{{x \to 0^+}} \frac{2}{3} x
   \]
   
4. **Evaluate the Limit:**  
   As \( x \) approaches 0 from the positive side, \( x \) tends to 0. Therefore:
   \[
   \frac{2}{3} x \to 0
   \]

The limit as \( x \) approaches infinity of \( \frac{2^x}{x^3} \) can be determined by recognizing that an exponential function grows much faster than any polynomial function.

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Identify the Problem:** We need to evaluate \( \lim_{{x \to \infty}} \frac{2^x}{x^3} \).

2. **Recognize Growth Rates:** The numerator is an exponential function, while the denominator is a polynomial of degree 3.

3. **Apply L’Hospital’s Rule (If Necessary):**
   - Differentiate both numerator and denominator repeatedly since we have \( \frac{\infty}{\infty} \) form.
   
4. **First Application:**
   - Numerator derivative: \( \frac{d}{dx}(2^x) = \ln(2) \cdot 2^x \)
   - Denominator derivative: \( \frac{d}{dx}(x^3) = 3x^2 \)
   - New limit: \( \lim_{{x \to \infty}} \frac{\ln(2) \cdot 2^x}{3x^2} \)

5. **Second Application:**
   - Numerator derivative: \( \ln(2) \cdot 2^x \)
   - Denominator derivative: \( 6x \)
   - New limit: \( \lim_{{x \to \infty}} \frac{(\ln(2))^2 \cdot 2^x}{6x} \)

6. **Third Application:**
   - Numerator derivative: \( (\ln(2))^3 \cdot 2^x \)
   - Denominator derivative: \( 6 \)
   - New limit: \( \lim_{{x \to \infty}} \frac{(\ln(2))^3 \cdot 2^x}{6} \)

7. **Evaluate the Final Limit:** As \( x \) approaches infinity, \( 2^x \) grows without bound, making the entire expression tend to infinity.

**Conclusion:** The limit is infinity because exponential growth outpaces polynomial growth in the denominator.

The limit evaluates to \(-\frac{1}{3}\) as shown by both the Taylor series expansion and applying L'Hospital's Rule.

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Problem Statement:** Find the limit as \( x \) approaches 0 of \( \frac{x - \tan x}{x^3} \).

2. **Approach Using Taylor Series Expansion:**
   - Expand \( \tan x \) around 0:
     \[
     \tan x = x + \frac{x^3}{3} + O(x^5)
     \]
   - Subtract from \( x \):
     \[
     x - \tan x = x - \left( x + \frac{x^3}{3} + \cdots \right) = -\frac{x^3}{3} + O(x^5)
     \]
   - Divide by \( x^3 \):
     \[
     \frac{x - \tan x}{x^3} = \frac{-\frac{x^3}{3} + O(x^5)}{x^3} = -\frac{1}{3} + O(x^2)
     \]
   - As \( x \to 0 \), the higher-order terms vanish, so:
     \[
     \lim_{x \to 0} \frac{x - \tan x}{x^3} = -\frac{1}{3}
     \]

3. **Alternative Approach Using L'Hospital's Rule:**
   - Recognize that both numerator and denominator approach 0 as \( x \to 0 \).
   - Apply L'Hospital's Rule:
     \[
     \lim_{x \to 0} \frac{x - \tan x}{x^3} = \lim_{x \to 0} \frac{1 - \sec^2 x}{3x^2}
     \]
   - Simplify using \( \sec^2 x = 1 + \tan^2 x \):
     \[
     \frac{1 - (1 + \tan^2 x)}{3x^2} = \frac{-\tan^2 x}{3x^2} = -\frac{\sin^2 x}{3x^2}
     \]
   - Use the known limit \( \lim_{x \to 0} \frac{\sin x}{x} = 1 \):
     \[
     -\frac{1}{3} \cdot \left( \lim_{x \to 0} \frac{\sin x}{x} \right)^2 = -\frac{1}{3}
     \]

4. **Verification with Taylor Series:**
   - Substitute the expansion of \( \tan x \) into the original limit:
     \[
     \frac{x - (x + \frac{x^3}{3} + \cdots)}{x^3} = \frac{-\frac{x^3}{3}}{x^3} = -\frac{1}{3}
     \]

5. **Conclusion:**
   - Both methods confirm that the limit is:
     \[
     \boxed{-\dfrac{1}{3}}
     \]

To evaluate the limit of \(\frac{1 + \cos\left(\frac{\pi}{x}\right)}{1 - \cos\left(\frac{\pi}{x}\right)}\) as \(x\) approaches infinity, we can proceed by analyzing the behavior of the trigonometric functions for small angles.

**Step 1: Substitute \( t = \frac{\pi}{x} \)**

As \( x \to \infty \), \( t \) becomes very small (approaching 0).

So, rewrite the expression in terms of \( t \):
\[
f(x) = \frac{1 + \cos(t)}{1 - \cos(t)}
\]

**Step 2: Use Taylor Series Expansion for Cosine**

For small angles \( t \), we can approximate:
\[
\cos(t) \approx 1 - \frac{t^2}{2} + \cdots
\]
Thus,
\[
1 + \cos(t) \approx 2 - \frac{t^2}{2}
\]
and
\[
1 - \cos(t) \approx \frac{t^2}{2}
\]

**Step 3: Substitute the Approximations into \( f(x) \)**

Substitute these approximations back into the function:
\[
f(x) \approx \frac{2 - \frac{t^2}{2}}{\frac{t^2}{2}} = \frac{4 - t^2}{t^2} = \frac{4}{t^2} - 1
\]

**Step 4: Express in Terms of \( x \)**

Recall that \( t = \frac{\pi}{x} \), so:
\[
f(x) \approx \frac{4}{\left(\frac{\pi}{x}\right)^2} - 1 = \frac{4x^2}{\pi^2} - 1
\]

**Step 5: Take the Limit as \( x \to \infty \)**

As \( x \) approaches infinity, the term \(-1\) becomes negligible compared to \(\frac{4x^2}{\pi^2}\). Therefore:
\[
\lim_{x \to \infty} f(x) = \lim_{x \to \infty} \left( \frac{4x^2}{\pi^2} - 1 \right) = \infty
\]

**Conclusion**

The limit of the given function as \( x \) approaches infinity is infinity.

\boxed{\infty}

The function \( f(x) = 3x^2 - 5x + 4 \).

To find its derivative:

1. Differentiate each term using the power rule:
   - The derivative of \( 3x^2 \) is \( 6x \).
   - The derivative of \( -5x \) is \( -5 \).
   - The derivative of the constant term \( 4 \) is \( 0 \).

Combining these, the derivative \( f'(x) = 6x - 5 \).

The derivative of \( \frac{1}{x} \) with respect to \( x \) can be found using basic differentiation rules.

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Express the Function Differently:**  
   The function given is \( f(x) = \frac{1}{x} \). This can be rewritten as \( f(x) = x^{-1} \).

2. **Apply the Power Rule:**  
   The power rule states that if \( f(x) = x^n \), then \( f'(x) = n \cdot x^{n-1} \). Here, \( n = -1 \).

3. **Differentiate:**  
   Using the power rule:
   \[
   f'(x) = (-1) \cdot x^{-1 - 1} = -x^{-2}
   \]

4. **Simplify the Result:**  
   Rewrite \( -x^{-2} \) as \( -\frac{1}{x^2} \).

**Final Answer:**
The derivative of \( \frac{1}{x} \) with respect to \( x \) is:
\[
-\frac{1}{x^2}
\]

The derivative of f(x) = tan(x) can be found using standard differentiation rules. Applying the quotient rule, we find that the derivative simplifies to sec²(x).

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Express tan(x):**
   \[
   \tan(x) = \frac{\sin(x)}{\cos(x)}
   \]

2. **Apply the Quotient Rule:**
   If \( f(x) = \frac{u}{v} \), then
   \[
   f'(x) = \frac{u'v - uv'}{v^2}
   \]
   
3. **Differentiate numerator and denominator:**
   - \( u = \sin(x) \Rightarrow u' = \cos(x) \)
   - \( v = \cos(x) \Rightarrow v' = -\sin(x) \)

4. **Plug into the Quotient Rule formula:**
   \[
   f'(x) = \frac{\cos(x)\cos(x) - \sin(x)(-\sin(x))}{\cos^2(x)}
   \]

5. **Simplify the numerator:**
   \[
   \cos^2(x) + \sin^2(x) = 1
   \]
   
6. **Final Simplification:**
   \[
   f'(x) = \frac{1}{\cos^2(x)} = \sec^2(x)
   \]

**Final Answer:**  
The derivative of \( \tan(x) \) is \( \sec^2(x) \).

To find the derivative of \( F(x) = \cos x \cdot \ln x \), we will use the **product rule**. The product rule states that if you have a function \( F(x) = u(x) \cdot v(x) \), then its derivative is:

\[
F'(x) = u'(x) \cdot v(x) + u(x) \cdot v'(x)
\]

Let's apply this to our function:

1. **Identify the components:**
   - Let \( u(x) = \cos x \)
   - Let \( v(x) = \ln x \)

   - The derivative of \( u(x) = \cos x \) is:

     \[

   \[

   \]

   \[

The derivative of \( x^x \) with respect to \( x \) can be found using logarithmic differentiation.

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Start with the function:**  
   Let \( y = x^x \).

2. **Take the natural logarithm of both sides:**  
   \( \ln(y) = \ln(x^x) \).

3. **Apply the power rule for logarithms:**  
   This simplifies to \( \ln(y) = x \cdot \ln(x) \).

4. **Differentiate both sides with respect to \( x \):**
   - Left side: \( \frac{d}{dx} [\ln(y)] = \frac{1}{y} \cdot \frac{dy}{dx} \).
   - Right side: Use the product rule on \( x \cdot \ln(x) \):
     - Let \( u = x \), so \( u' = 1 \).
     - Let \( v = \ln(x) \), so \( v' = \frac{1}{x} \).
     - Therefore, \( \frac{d}{dx}[x \cdot \ln(x)] = 1 \cdot \ln(x) + x \cdot \frac{1}{x} = \ln(x) + 1 \).

5. **Set the derivatives equal to each other:**  
   \( \frac{1}{y} \cdot \frac{dy}{dx} = \ln(x) + 1 \).

6. **Solve for \( \frac{dy}{dx} \):**  
   Multiply both sides by \( y \):
   \( \frac{dy}{dx} = y (\ln(x) + 1) \).

7. **Substitute back \( y = x^x \):**  
   \( \frac{dy}{dx} = x^x (\ln(x) + 1) \).

---

**Final Answer:**  
The derivative of \( x^x \) with respect to \( x \) is \( x^x (1 + \ln(x)) \).

To find the derivative of \( F(x) = x \cdot e^x \), we can use the **product rule** from calculus. The product rule states that if you have a function that is the product of two other functions, say \( u(x) \) and \( v(x) \), then the derivative of the product is:

\[
\frac{d}{dx}[u(x) \cdot v(x)] = u'(x) \cdot v(x) + u(x) \cdot v'(x)
\]

**Step 1: Identify the functions in the product**

In this case, let:
- \( u(x) = x \)
- \( v(x) = e^x \)

**Step 2: Differentiate each function separately**

First, find the derivative of \( u(x) \):
\[
u'(x) = \frac{d}{dx}(x) = 1
\]

Next, find the derivative of \( v(x) \):
\[
v'(x) = \frac{d}{dx}(e^x) = e^x
\]
(Recall that the derivative of \( e^x \) with respect to \( x \) is itself.)

**Step 3: Apply the product rule**

Now, plug these derivatives back into the product rule formula:
\[
F'(x) = u'(x) \cdot v(x) + u(x) \cdot v'(x)
\]
\[
F'(x) = 1 \cdot e^x + x \cdot e^x
\]

**Step 4: Simplify the expression**

Combine like terms:
\[
F'(x) = e^x + x \cdot e^x = (1 + x)e^x
\]

So, the derivative of \( F(x) = x \cdot e^x \) is:

\[
\boxed{(1 + x)e^{x}}
\]

To find the second derivative of \( F(x) = x^2 e^x \), we proceed as follows:

**Step 1: Find the First Derivative**

We use the product rule for differentiation, which states that if \( u(x) = x^2 \) and \( v(x) = e^x \), then:
\[
F'(x) = u'(x)v(x) + u(x)v'(x)
\]

Calculate the derivatives:
\[
u'(x) = 2x \quad \text{and} \quad v'(x) = e^x
\]

Substitute into the product rule formula:
\[
F'(x) = (2x)(e^x) + (x^2)(e^x) = e^x (2x + x^2)
\]

**Step 2: Find the Second Derivative**

Now, we differentiate \( F'(x) \) again using the product rule. Let:
\[
u(x) = 2x + x^2 \quad \text{and} \quad v(x) = e^x
\]

Calculate the derivatives:
\[
u'(x) = 2 + 2x \quad \text{and} \quad v'(x) = e^x
\]

Substitute into the product rule formula:
\[
F''(x) = (2 + 2x)(e^x) + (2x + x^2)(e^x)
\]

\[

- The first derivative of \( x^3 \) with respect to \( x \) is \( 3x^2 \).
- The first derivative of \( \ln x \) with respect to \( x \) is \( \frac{1}{x} \).

\[ F'(x) = 3x^2 \ln x + x^2 \]

- The derivative of \( 3x^2 \) is \( 6x \).
- The derivative of \( \ln x \) remains \( \frac{1}{x} \).

- The derivative is straightforward: \( 2x \).

To find the second derivative of the function \( F(x) = x^3 \ln x \), we'll follow these steps:

1. **Find the First Derivative** (\( F'(x) \)) using the product rule:
   
   The product rule states that if you have a function \( u(x)v(x) \), its derivative is:
   \[
   (u v)' = u'v + uv'
   \]
   
   Here, let:
   \[
   u = x^3 \quad \text{and} \quad v = \ln x
   \]
   
   Compute the derivatives:
   \[
   u' = 3x^2 \quad \text{and} \quad v' = \frac{1}{x}
   \]
   
   Applying the product rule:
   \[
   F'(x) = 3x^2 \ln x + x^3 \cdot \frac{1}{x} = 3x^2 \ln x + x^2
   \]

2. **Find the Second Derivative** (\( F''(x) \)) by differentiating \( F'(x) \):

   We have:
   \[
   F'(x) = 3x^2 \ln x + x^2
   \]
   
   Differentiate each term separately.

   - **First Term:** \( 3x^2 \ln x \)
     Apply the product rule again, where:
     \[
     u = 3x^2 \quad \text{and} \quad v = \ln x
     \]
     
     Compute the derivatives:
     \[
     u' = 6x \quad \text{and} \quad v' = \frac{1}{x}
     \]
     
     Applying the product rule:
     \[
     \frac{d}{dx}(3x^2 \ln x) = 6x \ln x + 3x
     \]
   
   - **Second Term:** \( x^2 \)
     The derivative is straightforward:
     \[
     \frac{d}{dx} (x^2) = 2x
     \]
   
   Combine these results:
   \[
   F''(x) = 6x \ln x + 3x + 2x = 6x \ln x + 5x
   \]

3. **Simplify the Expression** (if needed):
   \[
   F''(x) = x(6 \ln x + 5)
   \]

**Final Answer:**
\[
\boxed{x(6 \ln x + 5)}

Let \( y = \arctan(x) \), which implies \( \tan(y) = x \).

Differentiating both sides with respect to \( x \):
\[
\sec^2(y) \cdot \frac{dy}{dx} = 1
\]

Solving for \( \frac{dy}{dx} \):
\[
\frac{dy}{dx} = \frac{1}{\sec^2(y)}
\]

To find the derivative of \( F(x) = x \ln x \), we'll use the **product rule**. The product rule states that if you have a function \( f(x) = u(x) \cdot v(x) \), its derivative is:

\[
f'(x) = u'(x) \cdot v(x) + u(x) \cdot v'(x)
\]

For our function:
- Let \( u(x) = x \)
- Let \( v(x) = \ln x \)

First, find the derivatives of \( u(x) \) and \( v(x) \):
- \( u'(x) = 1 \)
- \( v'(x) = \frac{1}{x} \)

Now, apply the product rule:
\[
F'(x) = u'(x) \cdot v(x) + u(x) \cdot v'(x)
\]
\[

F'(x) = \ln x + 1
